Functional chimeric mRNAs encode proteins in mammalian immunity

Olivia Venezia, Harry Kane, Gang Du et al.. Functional chimeric mRNAs encode proteins in mammalian immunity. Nature. 2026 commentary, not primary research

This paper investigated whether mRNAs from different genes can undergo trans-splicing to generate chimeric transcripts that encode functional proteins during mammalian immune responses. Using a pipeline that combined long-read direct RNA sequencing with targeted and non-targeted validation in macrophages, the researchers identified chimeric transcripts produced through interchromosomal trans-splicing, with chromatin conformation capture revealing that inflammation promotes physical proximity between parent genes on different chromosomes. A key discovery was a chimeric mRNA fusing the pore-forming protein gasdermin D with a domain from Tmem106a, the expression of which is upregulated by inflammasome priming and whose encoded protein localizes to the plasma membrane. This chimeric protein interacts with canonical gasdermin D to accelerate and enhance pore formation and inflammatory cytokine release following inflammasome activation. In vivo experiments showed that this chimeric protein strikes a balance between host defence and immunopathology, with its loss protecting against lethal sepsis but impairing antibacterial defence, establishing that regulated chimeric mRNA formation is a biologically meaningful mechanism in inflammation and immunity.

Why it is getting attention: This work is attracting attention because it reveals that inflammation can physically bring together chromosomal regions to generate chimeric mRNAs that produce functional proteins, challenging conventional views of how genes operate independently during immune responses. The finding that these chimeric transcripts encode physiologically relevant proteins suggests a previously unrecognized layer of gene regulation in mammalian immunity.

3D epigenome of glial cell types in developing human cortex

Ian R. Jones, Li Wang, Michael Kosicki et al.. 3D epigenome of glial cell types in developing human cortex. Nature. 2026 commentary, not primary research

This study investigated the three-dimensional epigenomic landscape of glial cell types in the developing human cortex, focusing on four main populations — ventricular radial glia, outer radial glia, oligodendrocyte precursor cells, and microglia — sampled from mid-gestational neocortex. The researchers integrated multiple genomic modalities, including gene expression, chromatin accessibility, DNA methylation, and three-dimensional chromatin interaction data, to identify cell-type-specific candidate cis-regulatory elements and validate their function in transgenic mouse embryos. Using machine learning, the team prioritized schizophrenia risk variants residing within glial regulatory elements and confirmed in vivo that one specific risk allele disrupts a ventricular radial glia enhancer. The study also found that outer radial glia regulatory elements are enriched for human accelerated regions, and that a subset of these regions show activity differences compared to chimpanzee orthologues, with connections to genes involved in neuronal development. Together, these findings illuminate how cell-type-specific chromatin regulation in glial progenitors contributes to human cortical evolution and susceptibility to neuropsychiatric disorders.

Why it is getting attention: This work is attracting attention because it maps the three-dimensional epigenome of multiple glial cell types in the developing human cortex, revealing how non-coding genetic variants linked to neuropsychiatric disease and human-specific brain evolution are embedded within cell-type-specific regulatory architecture.

Creating bottom-up RNA transfer vehicles from synthetic protein assemblies

Maren Kirstin Schuhmacher, Christoph Gruber, Christopher Lang et al.. Creating bottom-up RNA transfer vehicles from synthetic protein assemblies. Nature. 2026 commentary, not primary research

This paper investigated whether artificial intelligence-designed protein assemblies, which exist outside of natural evolutionary trajectories, could be engineered into functional RNA delivery vehicles. The researchers combined natural protein domains with synthetic protein assemblies generated by generative AI models to construct a large library of RNA transfer vehicles with diverse sizes and shapes distinct from naturally occurring viral capsids. These engineered vehicles substantially outperformed widely used RNA delivery systems in transfer efficiency, and their cell-type targeting could be programmed by incorporating computationally designed peptide binders. The vehicles were validated across numerous cellular models and demonstrated safe in vivo biodistribution in a mouse at near-single-cell resolution. As a proof of therapeutic application, the vehicles were used to deliver gene-editing cargo to correct the genetic defect underlying Duchenne muscular dystrophy in both patient-derived cells and a pig model.

Why it is getting attention: This work is attracting attention because AI-designed synthetic protein assemblies can deliver RNA into cells more effectively than natural viruses, suggesting that bypassing evolutionary constraints allows for superior gene delivery vehicles.

Late-life semaglutide treatment slows ageing and extends lifespan in female mice

Yufan Feng, Marine Barthez, Yifei Wang et al.. Late-life semaglutide treatment slows ageing and extends lifespan in female mice. Nature. 2026 commentary, not primary research

This study investigated whether the GLP-1 receptor agonist semaglutide, a drug used to treat type 2 diabetes and obesity, could influence the ageing process and lifespan when administered late in life. Aged female mice were treated with semaglutide for several months, and its effects were compared directly against matched calorie restriction in a longitudinal study. Treatment with semaglutide improved physiological function, attenuated hallmarks of ageing, and modulated nutrient-sensing pathways and conserved genetic regulators of ageing, with continued treatment ultimately extending lifespan. Semaglutide recapitulated many benefits of calorie restriction but also produced superior outcomes in exploratory drive, spatial memory, and glucose control compared to calorie restriction alone. The authors conclude that GLP-1 receptor activation acts as a calorie restriction mimetic and propose this mechanism as a framework to explain the broad pleiotropic benefits of GLP-1 medicines beyond simple reductions in food intake.

Why it is getting attention: This work is attracting attention because it suggests that semaglutide, a drug already in widespread clinical use, can slow biological ageing and extend lifespan when given late in life, at least in female mice. The findings are notable because the treatment improved physiological function and reduced hallmarks of ageing, raising the possibility that existing medications could be repurposed to target the ageing process itself.

Robust inference and correlates from genetic associations with personality

Ted Schwaba, Margaret L. Clapp Sullivan, Wonuola A. Akingbuwa et al.. Robust inference and correlates from genetic associations with personality. Nature. 2026 commentary, not primary research

This paper investigated the genetic basis of the Big Five personality traits — extraversion, agreeableness, conscientiousness, neuroticism, and openness to experience — by conducting large-scale genome-wide association studies across numerous cohorts spanning over a million participants with European-like and African-like ancestry. The researchers performed meta-analyses of standard and within-family genetic association studies, and applied polygenic index, genetic correlation, and Mendelian randomization analyses to assess robustness, confounding, and downstream relevance of personality-associated genetic variants. They identified over a thousand lead genetic variants associated with personality, the majority of which were novel, and found that common genetic variants explain a modest but meaningful proportion of variance in each trait. Genetic associations were broadly consistent across geography, age, measurement instrument, and reporter type, and within-family analyses indicated that, unlike many other behavioural traits, personality genetics are minimally confounded by shared family environment. The authors conclude that the genetic architecture of personality is robustly generalizable and has widespread, potentially causal relevance to a range of important life behaviours and outcomes.

Why it is getting attention: This work is drawing attention because it demonstrates that the genetic basis of personality traits is broadly applicable across populations, largely free from confounding factors, and meaningfully connected to a wide range of human outcomes and experiences.

Exploring baryon semileptonic decays through polarization and entanglement

M. Ablikim, M. N. Achasov, P. Adlarson et al.. Exploring baryon semileptonic decays through polarization and entanglement. Nature. 2026 commentary, not primary research

This paper investigates hyperon semileptonic decays as an alternative means of precisely determining the CKM matrix element governing strange-to-up quark transitions, motivated by existing tensions between measurements from kaon and tau decays that may signal physics beyond the Standard Model. Previous experiments studying hyperon semileptonic decays lacked sufficient kinematic information to probe the relevant form factors effectively, so the researchers exploited the polarization and quantum entanglement of Lambda-antiLambda pairs produced at the J/ψ resonance to extract detailed decay information. Using this approach, they measured axial-vector and weak-magnetism couplings in the Lambda hyperon semileptonic decay, and obtained the first determinations of the absolute branching fraction and weak-electricity coupling for this channel. Combining their experimental results with recent lattice QCD calculations yielded a model-independent determination of the CKM element consistent with unitarity of the CKM matrix. The method substantially enhances single-event sensitivity and is broadly applicable to other baryon semileptonic decays, establishing a framework for a systematic research programme capable of achieving precision rivaling that of kaon decay measurements.

Why it is getting attention: By leveraging polarization and quantum entanglement measurements in baryon semileptonic decay, this work achieves greatly enhanced single-event sensitivity that, combined with theoretical calculations, yields a precise determination of a key quark-mixing matrix element. This provides an independent test of the standard model, making it notable for probing fundamental symmetries and potential physics beyond current theory.

Explainable deep learning improves human mental models of self-driving cars

Eoin M. Kenny, Akshay Dharmavaram, Sang Uk Lee et al.. Explainable deep learning improves human mental models of self-driving cars. Nature. 2026 commentary, not primary research

This paper investigated whether explainable artificial intelligence techniques could improve human understanding of self-driving car behavior in real-world conditions. The researchers developed the Concept-Wrapper Network, a method that grounds the reasoning of deep neural network-based planners in human-interpretable concepts while maintaining driving performance, and deployed it on an actual self-driving vehicle rather than in simulation. They found that the explanations generated by this approach enhanced the mental models of human drivers, enabling them to more accurately predict vehicle behavior, especially in unexpected or surprising scenarios. The work demonstrates that explainable deep learning can be both comprehensible and practically useful in realistic autonomous driving deployments, not merely in controlled or simulated settings. The authors propose that their approach could extend to other safety-critical autonomous systems and different neural network architectures, offering a validated pathway toward greater transparency and safety in autonomous agents.

Why it is getting attention: This work is attracting attention because it demonstrates that a concept-based explainability method, tested on an actual autonomous vehicle, measurably improves how well human drivers can understand and anticipate the vehicle's behavior—addressing a critical trust and safety challenge in real-world self-driving technology.

The nutritional value of invertebrate aquatic foods

Jessica Zamborain‐Mason, Nisha Marwaha, Seo‐Hyun Yoo et al.. The nutritional value of invertebrate aquatic foods. Nature. 2026 commentary, not primary research

This paper investigated the nutritional contributions of aquatic invertebrates to human diets, a food group whose dietary importance is often underappreciated. The researchers quantified the global nutrient supply provided by current aquatic invertebrate production and modeled the estimated nutrient content across tens of thousands of macroinvertebrate species. They found that these organisms are exceptionally rich in a wide range of micronutrients and fatty acids, with current production capable of meeting the annual requirements of billions of people for nutrients such as vitamin B12, selenium, copper, omega-3 fatty acids, iodine, and zinc. Nutrient profiles varied according to taxonomic group, environmental conditions, life-history traits, and consumption patterns. The authors argue that better integration of aquatic invertebrates into dietary guidelines, food policy, and sustainability assessments could yield significant public health and nutritional benefits globally.

Why it is getting attention: This work is drawing attention because it reveals that aquatic invertebrates are remarkably nutrient-rich, with the capacity to meet the annual vitamin B12 and selenium needs of the vast majority of the global population, highlighting their potential significance as a food source.

Intracellular complement factor H protects neurons during CNS inflammation

Christina Mayer, Marcel S. Woo, Jana K. Sonner et al.. Intracellular complement factor H protects neurons during CNS inflammation. Nature. 2026 commentary, not primary research

This study investigated why certain neurons are more resilient than others to degeneration during central nervous system inflammation, using multiple sclerosis as the disease context and retinal ganglion cells as a model system. Through single-nucleus RNA sequencing of retinal tissue from donors with multiple sclerosis and healthy controls, the researchers discovered that expression of complement factor H strongly correlated with neuronal survival. Experimentally, they found that complement factor H is induced in neurons under inflammatory and oxidative stress conditions, where it functions intracellularly at the endoplasmic reticulum to limit reactive oxygen species accumulation and lipid peroxidation, thereby protecting against ferroptosis. Critically, this protective function depended on a specific protein domain and operated independently of complement factor H's known secreted form and canonical complement pathway activity, revealing a non-canonical intracellular role for this protein. These findings identify complement factor H as a key mediator of neuronal resilience across the central nervous system in both mice and humans, with potential implications for neuroprotective therapies in multiple sclerosis.

Why it is getting attention: This work is drawing attention because it identifies a complement protein operating inside neurons—rather than in the bloodstream—as a key protector of neuronal resilience during central nervous system inflammation, with findings shown to be relevant in both mice and humans.

Xiao-Yu Li, Yu‐Chen Cai, Zhao‐Dong Meng et al.. Probing far-from-equilibrium dynamics of electrical double layers. Nature. 2026 commentary, not primary research

Abstract unavailable. Open the link to read more.

The integrated experimental and computational framework enables real-time tracking of the structure and evolution of electrical double layers, offering fundamental new insight into electrochemical processes.

Structure and operating principles of a monkeypox virus replisome

Zishuo Yu, Pradeep Sathyanarayana, Joel M. J. Tan et al.. Structure and operating principles of a monkeypox virus replisome. Nature. 2026 commentary, not primary research

This study investigated how the monkeypox virus assembles a functional replisome to replicate its genome, focusing on the interaction between the viral polymerase and the helicase-primase complex. Using cryo-electron microscopy, the researchers determined structures of DNA-bound replisomes containing the three-subunit polymerase holoenzyme together with the hexameric helicase-primase. They found that replisome assembly triggers large-scale conformational changes in the helicase-primase, causing two of its primase domains to interact directly with specific polymerase subunits. Biochemical and single-molecule experiments demonstrated that this conformational rearrangement simultaneously activates helicase activity and enhances primase activity, resolving how the previously autoinhibited helicase becomes activated. These findings reveal the structural and mechanistic basis by which monkeypox virus coordinates helicase and polymerase functions during DNA replication, with implications for understanding a medically important class of pathogens.

Why it is getting attention: This work is attracting attention because it reveals the molecular architecture and mechanistic details of how the monkeypox virus copying machinery assembles and activates, showing that dramatic structural rearrangements in a key enzyme drive its activation through specific interactions with the polymerase complex. Understanding these principles could inform the development of targeted antiviral strategies against monkeypox virus.

Abstract unavailable. Open the link to read more.

Blood cells replace the immune cells of the ageing human brain

Blood cells replace the immune cells of the ageing human brain. Nature. 2026 commentary, not primary research

Research examined whether microglia — the brain's resident immune cells, established at birth — truly persist for life without outside contribution, as previously thought. Using mutational analysis and cell lineage tracing approaches, the work investigated the origins of immune cells found in the aging human brain. The findings demonstrate that bone marrow-derived circulating blood cells colonize the brain during aging, replacing the original microglia population. This reveals a previously unrecognized dynamic interaction between the peripheral immune system and the brain that challenges the long-held view of microglial self-sufficiency throughout life.

Why it is getting attention: This work is attracting attention because it overturns the long-held assumption that the brain's resident immune cells persist for life without outside reinforcement, demonstrating instead that circulating blood-derived immune cells infiltrate and replace them during ageing. The finding reveals an unexpected, previously unrecognized connection between the aging brain and the broader immune system.

Aquatic invertebrates contribute substantially to global nutrient supplies

Aquatic invertebrates contribute substantially to global nutrient supplies. Nature. 2026 commentary, not primary research

This paper examined the nutritional content of aquatic invertebrate species to assess their contribution to global nutrient supplies relevant to human health. The analysis focused on groups such as aquatic molluscs and crustaceans, which are frequently overlooked in discussions of food and nutrition. The findings indicate that global production of these invertebrates represents a substantial and rich source of nutrients critical to human health. The authors argue that their work provides a foundation for greater recognition of aquatic invertebrate nutrition, and call for these contributions to be integrated into conservation, management, and policy frameworks.

Why it is getting attention: This work is attracting attention because it reveals that aquatic invertebrates represent a substantial and previously underappreciated source of nutrients critical to human health. The findings are seen as a foundation for integrating these invertebrate nutritional contributions into conservation, management, and policy frameworks.

Early Detection and Surveillance of the SARS-CoV-2 Variant BA.3.2 — Worldwide, November 2024–February 2026

Mila Shakya, Kevin C. Ma, Laura J. Hughes et al.. Early Detection and Surveillance of the SARS-CoV-2 Variant BA.3.2 — Worldwide, November 2024–February 2026. MMWR Morbidity and Mortality Weekly Report. 2026

This paper examined the emergence and early spread of the SARS-CoV-2 variant BA.3.2, which was first identified in South Africa in late 2024. The authors employed a multimodal genomic surveillance approach, drawing on traveler-based genomic surveillance, clinical specimen testing, airplane wastewater analysis, and community wastewater monitoring to track the variant's international and domestic spread. BA.3.2 carries a large number of substitutions and deletions in its spike protein relative to the antigens used in current COVID-19 vaccines, raising concern that it may reduce protection conferred by prior infection or vaccination. Surveillance detected the variant across dozens of countries, and within the United States it was found in travelers, clinical patients, and wastewater samples spanning a substantial number of states. The authors conclude that continued genomic surveillance is essential to monitor the ongoing evolution of SARS-CoV-2 and assess the public health implications of variants like BA.3.2.

Metagenomic Sequencing for Wastewater-Based Surveillance: Modeling and Experimental Approaches for Determining Limit of Detection

Xiao A, Besse K, Connors D et al.. Metagenomic Sequencing for Wastewater-Based Surveillance: Modeling and Experimental Approaches for Determining Limit of Detection. 2026

This paper investigated the use of metagenomic next-generation sequencing as a tool for wastewater-based surveillance, focusing on understanding its limit of detection and related performance characteristics that have limited its broader adoption compared to PCR-based methods. The researchers developed a computational model to estimate operational detection limits for various sequencing approaches within a simplified local wastewater system based on a military installation, and they conducted two complementary types of experiments using a bacterial organism spiked either into wastewater samples in the laboratory or directly into a real-world wastewater system at a source location. The findings demonstrate that metagenomic sequencing detection performance depends substantially on the choice of sequencing method and the data analysis pipeline employed. The study also found that laboratory-spiked experiments do not fully replicate the conditions encountered in system-spiked experiments, highlighting the importance of site-specific method characterization. The authors conclude that because wastewater systems differ in configuration, background microbial communities, and sequencing inhibitors, implementations of metagenomic sequencing for surveillance should be validated individually at each site.

Wastewater Surveillance for Early Warning of Infectious Disease Outbreaks: A Systematic Review of Evidence and Implications for One Health Surveillance.

Panigrahi S, Pattnaik M, Pradhan R et al.. Wastewater Surveillance for Early Warning of Infectious Disease Outbreaks: A Systematic Review of Evidence and Implications for One Health Surveillance.. Pathogens (Basel, Switzerland). 2026

This systematic review examined the role of wastewater surveillance in providing early warning of infectious disease outbreaks and its alignment with One Health principles, which integrate human, animal, and environmental health monitoring. The authors searched multiple major databases and screened hundreds of studies, ultimately synthesizing findings from those meeting strict inclusion criteria. The review found a consistent positive association between pathogen detection in wastewater and clinical case counts in communities, with respiratory and gastrointestinal pathogens predominating and seasonal patterns observed across identified organisms. Targeted sampling in socially vulnerable areas and appropriate collection frequency were identified as particularly valuable for early outbreak detection. However, the review also revealed that few studies have explicitly operationalized a One Health framework, and the authors call for standardized surveillance methods and stronger integration of human, animal, and environmental monitoring systems in future wastewater surveillance programmes.

International Airport Wastewater as a Sentinel Site for Genomic Surveillance of Human Viruses and Bacteriophages.

Carvalho APA, Almada MS, Leal CD et al.. International Airport Wastewater as a Sentinel Site for Genomic Surveillance of Human Viruses and Bacteriophages.. Microorganisms. 2026

This study investigated whether international airport wastewater could serve as a sentinel site for detecting human-associated viruses and bacteriophages, addressing a gap in metagenomic surveillance research in South America. Researchers collected untreated wastewater samples from a major Brazilian airport over a period spanning late 2021 to early 2023 and analyzed them using hybrid-capture target-enriched next-generation sequencing to characterize the virome. The analysis identified hundreds of viral and bacteriophage-associated taxa, including numerous human-associated viruses spanning multiple viral families, with norovirus detected in every sample and SARS-CoV-2 co-detected alongside dengue virus in one collection. Bacteriophage profiling revealed dozens of host-associated phage groups, with those linked to Streptococcus predominating, offering indirect signatures of the microbial communities carried by the traveling population. The authors conclude that airport wastewater effectively captures diverse pathogen signals reflective of population mobility and has strong potential as an environmental genomic surveillance tool for early warning of emerging and circulating pathogens.

Clade-specific multiplex digital PCR assay for monkeypox virus detection in spike-in wastewater samples.

Pitton M, Gan C, Schmidhalter P et al.. Clade-specific multiplex digital PCR assay for monkeypox virus detection in spike-in wastewater samples.. Access microbiology. 2026

This paper developed and validated a multiplex digital PCR assay for detecting and quantifying monkeypox virus in wastewater samples. The study focused on distinguishing between the major clades and subclades of the virus, which is epidemiologically important because these clades differ in transmissibility and mortality. The researchers validated a four-plex digital PCR approach using spike-in wastewater samples, evaluating its linearity, sensitivity, and specificity. The assay demonstrated strong analytical performance across all targets, with limits of detection in the low single-digit copies per reaction range, and successfully differentiated between viral clades. The authors propose this tool as valuable for wastewater-based epidemiological surveillance, offering a population-level monitoring approach that is independent of individual clinical testing.

Inferring variant-specific effective reproduction numbers from combined case and sequencing data

Marlin D Figgins, Trevor Bedford. Inferring variant-specific effective reproduction numbers from combined case and sequencing data. eLife. 2026

This paper addresses the challenge of accurately estimating the transmission rates of co-circulating SARS-CoV-2 variants by jointly analyzing confirmed case counts and genetic sequencing data. The authors extended existing methods for inferring effective reproduction numbers to simultaneously estimate variant-specific effective reproduction numbers and variant frequencies across states in the United States over a period spanning early 2021 to early 2022. Their approach allows structured relationships between reproduction numbers to be inferred across time series, enabling estimation of fixed variant-specific growth advantages that remain consistent across geographic locations. Applying this framework to variants of concern and variants of interest, they demonstrate that using case data alongside sequence proportions captures richer epidemiological dynamics than sequencing data alone. The method yields estimates of consistent growth advantages for specific variants across different locations, providing a more complete picture of variant transmission than frequency-based approaches.

Mobile Genetic Elements as Central Drivers of Antimicrobial Resistance: Molecular Mechanisms, Evolutionary Ecology, One Health Implications and Control Strategies

Hemayet Hossain, Md. Hasan Ali, Tanvir Ahmad et al.. Mobile Genetic Elements as Central Drivers of Antimicrobial Resistance: Molecular Mechanisms, Evolutionary Ecology, One Health Implications and Control Strategies. Antibiotics. 2026

This review examines the central role of mobile genetic elements in driving the global spread of antimicrobial resistance across bacterial species and ecosystems. The authors synthesized current knowledge by critically evaluating recent genomic, epidemiological, and ecological studies alongside case studies of clinically significant resistance outbreaks, covering diverse elements including plasmids, integrons, transposons, insertion sequences, integrative and conjugative elements, and prophages. The review finds that these elements act as hubs for the capture, recombination, and stabilization of antibiotic resistance genes, enabling the emergence of multidrug-resistant and extensively drug-resistant pathogens. Ecological pressures such as antibiotics, heavy metals, and biocides are identified as drivers that influence mobile genetic element dynamics, while One Health transmission pathways connect resistance dissemination across human, animal, and environmental compartments. The authors argue that linking these molecular insights to applied interventions — including genomic surveillance, mobile genetic element-targeted inhibitors, phage therapy, and CRISPR-based strategies — is essential for developing effective responses to the antimicrobial resistance crisis.

The WHO priority list of antibiotic-resistant bacteria: challenges and opportunities for next-generation antimicrobial development

Emad M. Abdallah, Abdulrahman Mohammed Alhudhaibi, Mahmoud M. Dahab et al.. The WHO priority list of antibiotic-resistant bacteria: challenges and opportunities for next-generation antimicrobial development. Frontiers in Pharmacology. 2026

This narrative review examines the global threat posed by antimicrobial resistance, focusing on the bacterial pathogens designated as priorities by the World Health Organization to guide drug development and research investment. The authors synthesize current understanding of the molecular mechanisms by which these pathogens evade existing antibiotics, encompassing reduced membrane permeability, efflux pump overexpression, enzymatic inactivation, target modification, biofilm formation, and horizontal gene transfer. The review critically evaluates why conventional antibiotic discovery pipelines have largely failed to keep pace with resistance, and assesses a range of emerging therapeutic strategies—including artificial intelligence-assisted drug discovery, phage therapy, antimicrobial peptides, CRISPR-based approaches, and natural product-derived compounds—with attention to their translational challenges, toxicity, dosing, and regulatory hurdles. The authors also emphasize the importance of antimicrobial stewardship, global surveillance, and a One Health framework that integrates human, animal, and environmental health sectors. The overall conclusion is that confronting priority resistant pathogens will require multidisciplinary approaches that connect molecular biology, pharmacology, clinical translation, and public health.

Disinfection in wastewater treatment: From conventional to emerging technologies

Mariana Costa, Daniel Terrón, Cristiano Leal et al.. Disinfection in wastewater treatment: From conventional to emerging technologies. Journal of Water Process Engineering. 2026

This review paper examines the full landscape of wastewater disinfection technologies, from established methods to emerging alternatives, in the context of growing challenges facing treatment plants. The authors critically synthesize how conventional approaches such as chlorination, ultraviolet radiation, and ozonation work and where they fall short, particularly regarding antibiotic-resistant bacteria and genes, complex wastewater matrices, and the formation of harmful disinfection byproducts. Special focus is placed on advanced oxidation processes and hybrid systems, which can simultaneously inactivate microorganisms and remove micropollutants. The review also addresses the integration of real-time monitoring and artificial intelligence tools for predicting disinfection performance and optimizing chemical use. The authors conclude that combining advanced treatment technologies with intelligent monitoring systems will be essential for achieving microbial safety, byproduct control, energy efficiency, and economic viability under increasingly stringent regulatory requirements for wastewater reuse.

Postbiotics and phage synergy in precision oral microbiome engineering: systems biology strategies targeting Streptococcus mutans in dental caries

Guhanraj Radhamanalan. Postbiotics and phage synergy in precision oral microbiome engineering: systems biology strategies targeting Streptococcus mutans in dental caries. Frontiers in Molecular Biosciences. 2026

This review examines the potential of postbiotics as precision tools for managing dental caries by targeting the oral microbiome and virome, with particular focus on the pathogenic bacterium Streptococcus mutans. The authors synthesize current knowledge on how non-viable microbial products and metabolic derivatives—including organic acids, bacteriocins, exopolysaccharides, and structural components from inactivated probiotic cells—can reduce biofilm acidogenicity, disrupt bacterial communication, weaken extracellular matrix integrity, and modulate mucosal immune responses while preserving commensal microbial balance. The review further explores synergistic strategies combining postbiotics with bacteriophage-based therapies, nanoformulations, and bioadhesive delivery systems to improve site-specific efficacy against cariogenic biofilms. The authors highlight how multi-omics technologies, systems biology modeling, and artificial intelligence-driven diagnostics could enable personalized interventions tailored to individual oral microbial signatures, and note that postbiotic modulation of viral-bacterial interactions may help curb antimicrobial resistance dissemination. The review concludes by identifying key translational obstacles—including formulation stability, safety evaluation, regulatory pathways, and comprehensive virome profiling—that must be addressed before these approaches can reach clinical application.

Two extremely irradiated volatile-rich sub-Neptunes with companions in the TOI-426 and TOI-1839 systems: Insights into arrival and survival near the lower edge of the Neptunian desert

A. Castro-González, O. Barragán, D. J. Armstrong et al.. Two extremely irradiated volatile-rich sub-Neptunes with companions in the TOI-426 and TOI-1839 systems: Insights into arrival and survival near the lower edge of the Neptunian desert. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This study confirmed two highly irradiated sub-Neptune planets situated near the lower boundary of the Neptunian desert, a region of orbital parameter space where planets are rarely found, using space-based photometry and a large set of ground-based radial velocity measurements. The researchers characterized both planets' bulk properties and modeled stellar activity contamination, also detecting additional companions in each system, including a long-period giant planet in one system and a second transiting sub-Neptune in the other. Despite receiving extraordinarily high levels of stellar irradiation, the densities of all three transiting planets require substantial volatile content, and atmospheric escape modeling strongly favors water-dominated rather than hydrogen/helium envelopes, pointing to a steam-world composition that can survive on billion-year timescales. A population-level analysis revealed a striking statistical excess of outer giant companions specifically among low-mass planets residing in the predicted tidal survival band near the desert's lower edge, compared to those outside it. These findings together suggest that the lower boundary of the Neptunian desert is shaped not solely by photoevaporation of primordial envelopes but also significantly by high-eccentricity tidal migration driven by outer giant companions.

Ion abundances in the plasma tail of 3I/ATLAS show that it is N2-rich

Lea Ferellec, Cyrielle Opitom, Colin Snodgrass. Ion abundances in the plasma tail of 3I/ATLAS show that it is N2-rich. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper studied the chemical composition of the third interstellar object, 3I/ATLAS, by analyzing ion emissions in its plasma tail. Using integral-field unit spectroscopy with the WHT/WEAVE-LIFU instrument on two dates following perihelion, the researchers detected multiple ionic species — including N2+, CO+, CO2+, H2O+, and CH+ — concentrated in an anti-solar tail. By applying fluorescence factors to the observed ion abundances, they derived abundance ratios among these species and used the N2+/CO+ ratio to establish a lower limit on the N2/CO ratio in the comet. The results indicate that 3I/ATLAS is significantly enriched in nitrogen gas relative to Solar System comets, a characteristic interpreted as evidence for cold formation conditions in its home planetary system. The overall ion abundance pattern is also consistent with a general enrichment in hypervolatiles, and only a marginal spatial variation — a decrease in CH+ with distance along the tail — was detected.

Propagation Model for SSC attacks: Why SBOM (tools) don't tell the whole truth

Ljubica Grgic, Lazar Maksimovic, Pavel Laskov. Propagation Model for SSC attacks: Why SBOM (tools) don't tell the whole truth. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper investigates the limitations of Software Bill of Materials (SBOM) tools in securing software supply chains, arguing that these tools fail to capture how vulnerabilities propagate through an entire chain. The authors developed a four-stage propagation model designed to assess how security weaknesses spread from structural exposure through to taint path analysis. They empirically evaluated four open-source SBOM tools against this model using three software projects and the Log4j vulnerability as a case study. Their findings reveal that current SBOM tools systematically address only the first two stages of the model, covering structural exposure and vulnerability class presence, while lacking the capabilities needed for code reachability analysis and taint path analysis. The authors conclude that centering software supply chain security research on propagation effects is essential to prevent localized cyber risks from escalating into broader systemic risks.

Coincidence-based spectral engineering for spectral matching in cascaded downconversion

Mohammed Charafi, Alexandre Z. Leger, Samridhi Gambhir et al.. Coincidence-based spectral engineering for spectral matching in cascaded downconversion. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper investigates the generation of three-photon entangled states through cascaded spontaneous parametric downconversion, where a key challenge is achieving spectral compatibility between successive nonlinear optical stages. The authors demonstrate that by filtering the herald photon in coincidence with detection, one can conditionally reshape the spectrum of its entangled partner, tailoring it to match the acceptance bandwidth of the second nonlinear stage without modifying the sources themselves. They employed an electro-optically gated spectrometer to directly measure the conditional spectra governing the cascaded process, enabling quantitative predictions of the enhancement in conversion probability per detected herald. Photon-triplet measurements were then used to verify these predictions, confirming improved performance at fixed heralding rates. The work establishes coincidence-based spectral engineering as a practical method for overcoming spectral incompatibility in cascaded downconversion, especially in scenarios constrained by detector saturation or mismatched source bandwidths.

When LLM Decompilers Recompile More and Preserve Less

Chang Liu, Edward Raff, Kristopher Micinski. When LLM Decompilers Recompile More and Preserve Less. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper investigates whether current evaluation metrics for large language model-based decompilers—specifically recompilability and re-executability—adequately capture decompilation quality. The authors demonstrate that these metrics can be misleading: a decompiled function may successfully build and pass all shipped tests while diverging from the original binary's behavior on other inputs, and security-relevant crashes from known vulnerabilities may silently disappear from the recompiled code. To address this, they introduce Decompile-Diverge, a behavioral comparison oracle that automatically synthesizes test drivers, grows fuzzing corpora from the reference binary, and checks whether the decompiled code produces matching behavior across those inputs. Applying this framework to established benchmarks, real library functions, and vulnerability-grounded test cases, they find that functions passing all shipped tests still exhibit behavioral divergence at meaningful rates, and that LLM refinement can simultaneously improve build rates while reducing behavioral fidelity and suppressing crash-triggering behavior in vulnerable functions. Source-level analysis traces these divergences to LLM-introduced fields, types, callees, and guards that silently resolve the ambiguities that traditional decompilers leave as visible placeholders.

Dust Substructures and Line Perturbations driven by a Forming Planet in J16120

Anibal Sierra, Andrés F. Izquierdo, Paola Pinilla et al.. Dust Substructures and Line Perturbations driven by a Forming Planet in J16120. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This study investigated the protoplanetary disc around a young star for evidence of active planet formation, building on prior hints from multiple observational wavelengths. Using high-resolution millimeter and submillimeter observations from ALMA, the researchers mapped dust continuum emission and molecular line emission to characterize both the disc structure and gas dynamics. Analysis of the dust continuum revealed an inner disc and two dust rings, with the overall morphology better described by an eccentric disc model than a symmetric one. Examination of gas kinematics uncovered a localized increase in velocity dispersion, kink-like features, a shift between sub- and super-Keplerian velocities, and an arc-like residual structure, all spatially coincident with a previously identified circumplanetary disc candidate. These kinematic signatures are consistent with predictions from planet–disc interaction simulations and, together with the dust morphology, provide compelling evidence that a planet is actively forming within the disc gap.

Research progress on nucleic acid amplification–based detection technologies for phytopathogenic fungi

Baoyu Wang, Mei Liu, Hui Wang et al.. Research progress on nucleic acid amplification–based detection technologies for phytopathogenic fungi. Applied Microbiology and Biotechnology. 2026

This review paper examines the threat posed by phytopathogenic fungi to global agriculture, noting that fungal pathogens account for the vast majority of plant disease infections and are responsible for substantial annual crop yield losses worldwide. The authors surveyed the principles and recent advances of nucleic acid amplification-based detection technologies as applied to identifying these pathogens. The technologies reviewed span thermal cycling approaches such as conventional PCR, real-time quantitative PCR, and droplet digital PCR, as well as isothermal amplification methods including loop-mediated isothermal amplification and recombinase polymerase amplification. The review also covers CRISPR/Cas-assisted detection systems that couple isothermal amplification with CRISPR diagnostics, highlighting their potential for field-deployable, low-instrumentation use. The authors evaluate the strengths and limitations of each approach, arguing that integrated molecular detection workflows can support early diagnosis and precision management of phytopathogenic fungal diseases.

Rapid and Sensitive Detection of &lt;i&gt;Schistosoma mansoni&lt;/i&gt; in the Intermediate Snail Hosts Using Loop-Mediated Isothermal Amplification (LAMP) Diagnostics.

Li HM, Qin ZQ, Lv S et al.. Rapid and Sensitive Detection of &lt;i&gt;Schistosoma mansoni&lt;/i&gt; in the Intermediate Snail Hosts Using Loop-Mediated Isothermal Amplification (LAMP) Diagnostics.. Tropical medicine and infectious disease. 2026

The study addressed the need for improved detection of *Schistosoma mansoni* infection in its intermediate snail hosts, which is critical for schistosomiasis control but poorly served by conventional parasitological methods. Researchers developed a loop-mediated isothermal amplification assay targeting the mitochondrial NADH dehydrogenase subunit 1 gene of *S. mansoni*, optimizing it for a simple, single-temperature reaction, and evaluated it against microscopy, conventional PCR, and real-time PCR using both laboratory-infected snails and field-collected samples from two African countries. The new assay achieved perfect specificity and high sensitivity in laboratory conditions, outperforming microscopy and matching real-time PCR performance. In field surveys, it detected a higher proportion of infected snails than conventional PCR, while microscopy failed to identify any positive individuals, highlighting its superior sensitivity in real-world settings. The authors conclude that this assay represents a sensitive, specific, and practically deployable tool for routine surveillance of snail infection and early detection of changes in schistosomiasis transmission.

Dual readout RT-LAMP for rapid on-site dengue virus detection and serotyping.

Brambilla R, Rockwood N, Michel J et al.. Dual readout RT-LAMP for rapid on-site dengue virus detection and serotyping.. Microbiology spectrum. 2026

This study addressed the need for rapid, field-deployable dengue virus diagnostics by developing four serotype-specific reverse transcription loop-mediated isothermal amplification assays. Primer design was guided by analysis of thousands of whole-genome sequences spanning diverse geographical regions, with degenerate bases incorporated to maximize coverage of global viral genetic diversity while limiting the number of distinct oligonucleotides required. The assays operate at constant temperature, eliminating the need for thermal cyclers, and feature a dual readout system allowing both real-time fluorescence monitoring and simple visual colorimetric interpretation without instruments. Clinical evaluation using PCR-confirmed patient samples covering serotypes one through three showed strong detection rates, with complete detection achieved for one serotype, and no cross-reactivity was observed between serotypes. Results are obtained within forty to sixty minutes, making these assays well suited for point-of-care deployment in resource-limited, dengue-endemic settings.

Broad-Temperature Polymerase in Nucleic Acid Amplification-Based Diagnostics: From Thermal Precision to Dynamic Conditions.

Hamidizadeh M, Bier FF. Broad-Temperature Polymerase in Nucleic Acid Amplification-Based Diagnostics: From Thermal Precision to Dynamic Conditions.. ACS sensors. 2026

This paper investigated nucleic acid amplification methods that can function across a wide range of temperatures, addressing the limitations of conventional approaches that require precise thermal control. The researchers developed a LAMP-based amplification system using a Bst polymerase with strong strand displacement activity and broad thermostability, enabling DNA synthesis across a temperature range from below typical LAMP conditions to temperatures approaching those used in PCR. They demonstrated that amplification could be initiated at both high and low starting temperatures and sustained even under dynamically changing temperature profiles rather than fixed conditions. The system was validated for detecting SARS-CoV-2 and mycobacterial genomic DNA, confirming its diagnostic utility. The authors propose this broad-temperature amplification platform as a promising tool for point-of-care and field diagnostics where maintaining strict thermal regulation is impractical.

The analysis of natural gemstones and their synthetic counterparts using analytical spectroscopy methods

Raabell Shah. The analysis of natural gemstones and their synthetic counterparts using analytical spectroscopy methods. Research Output (Edinburgh Napier University). 2026

This study examined the use of Raman spectroscopy to distinguish natural gemstones from their laboratory-grown synthetic counterparts, focusing on ruby, sapphire, emerald, and spinel, which are among the most commercially significant gems. The researchers evaluated both a laboratory-grade Raman spectrometer equipped with two different laser wavelengths and a portable Raman system, applying them to identify gems produced by various manufacturing methods and to determine geographic origin. The laboratory instrument proved capable of differentiating natural from synthetic gemstones and determining the origin of same-coloured stones, with the dual-laser capability being essential since emerald analysis required a specific laser wavelength. The portable system could distinguish between different stone types but lacked the sensitivity needed to discriminate between natural and synthetic specimens. Repeated measurements confirmed the reliability of Raman spectroscopy as a non-destructive, preparation-free analytical tool for gemological identification, with measured spectral bands consistently falling within accepted tolerances of reference values.

Porous material engineering through synthesis for smart sensor systems

Se Jin Choi, Sang Yoon Park, Kang Hyeon Kim et al.. Porous material engineering through synthesis for smart sensor systems. Microsystems & Nanoengineering. 2026

This review examines porous materials as a platform for next-generation sensor systems, focusing on how their high surface areas, tunable pore structures, and chemical functionalization enable improved analyte detection. The authors systematically survey representative categories of porous materials—including metal oxides, polymers, and carbon-based systems—alongside their synthesis routes, which encompass sol-gel processes, template-assisted methods, three-dimensional printing, and light-material interactions. Fundamental sensing mechanisms are analyzed across electrical, electrochemical, and optical transduction pathways that porous architectures make possible. The review covers applications spanning environmental monitoring, biomedical diagnostics, and smart packaging, where porous material-based sensors are reported to show faster response times, better analyte discrimination, and greater operational stability. The authors draw together design principles and fabrication strategies intended to guide future development and practical deployment of advanced sensing technologies.

Viral metagenomics of synanthropic urban bats: A surveillance strategy for uncovering potentially zoonotic viruses

Juliana Amorim Conselheiro, Filipe Romero Rebello Moreira, Gisely Toledo Barone et al.. Viral metagenomics of synanthropic urban bats: A surveillance strategy for uncovering potentially zoonotic viruses. One Health. 2026

This study developed a viral surveillance framework using Brazil's existing rabies passive surveillance program to detect bat-borne viruses with zoonotic potential. Researchers applied a selection algorithm to choose representative specimens from thousands of collected bats across São Paulo state, then subjected paired lung and intestine samples to nanopore metagenomic sequencing. The analysis revealed viral genetic sequences spanning multiple virus families of public health concern, including coronaviruses, arenaviruses, and paramyxoviruses, demonstrating substantial previously undetected viral diversity in urban synanthropic bats. Notably, the approach uncovered a previously unknown filovirus in bats from the Americas, highlighting the framework's capacity for identifying novel potential pathogens. The framework was validated by multisectoral One Health experts as a scalable and cost-effective model suitable for national expansion and adaptable to low- and middle-income country settings.

Advances in low-cost electrochemical sensors for healthcare monitoring and diagnostics

B. Anuthra, Jasmine Ratan, Priyanka Gupta et al.. Advances in low-cost electrochemical sensors for healthcare monitoring and diagnostics. Advances in Industrial and Engineering Chemistry. 2026

This review examines recent progress in the development of low-cost electrochemical sensors designed for healthcare diagnostics and point-of-care testing, particularly in resource-limited settings. The authors survey innovations in materials, design, and fabrication approaches, with emphasis on cost-reduction strategies such as the use of carbon-based compounds, conductive polymers, and plant-derived bioactive molecules, as well as scalable manufacturing techniques like screen printing and three-dimensional printing. The review covers applications of these sensors in monitoring and diagnosing chronic and infectious conditions including diabetes, cardiovascular diseases, and infectious diseases, as well as their integration with digital platforms for real-time data collection. The authors also address outstanding challenges including sensor stability, specificity, and barriers to large-scale commercialization, alongside proposed solutions and future research directions. Overall, the review argues that affordable electrochemical sensors hold considerable potential to broaden access to diagnostics, lower healthcare costs, and improve patient outcomes.

Tracing the Cosmic Origins: Machine Learning Reconstruction of the Primordial Density Field from EoR Observations

Anchal Saxena, P. Daniel Meerburg, Guochao Sun et al.. Tracing the Cosmic Origins: Machine Learning Reconstruction of the Primordial Density Field from EoR Observations. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper investigates whether the primordial density field of the Universe can be reconstructed from observations of large-scale structure during the Epoch of Reionization, aiming to recover cosmological information lost to non-linear gravitational evolution and astrophysical processes. The researchers used simulated 21-cm and CO line-intensity maps generated with a dedicated simulation framework, feeding these into a three-dimensional U-Net neural network trained to reconstruct the initial density field at very high redshift. The two emission lines were chosen because they trace complementary environments — 21-cm probing diffuse neutral gas and CO probing dense star-forming regions — and realistic instrumental effects mimicking next-generation survey telescopes were incorporated. In the idealized case, combining both tracers yielded high cross-correlation with the true initial conditions across a broad range of scales, and even under realistic observational noise the joint reconstruction maintained meaningful fidelity at large scales. Crucially, performing simulation-based inference on power-spectrum summaries derived from the reconstructed fields demonstrated that this approach roughly doubles the precision on key cosmological parameters such as the amplitude of matter fluctuations and the spectral index, confirming that joint multi-tracer reconstruction can recover otherwise inaccessible primordial information from future surveys.

WearableQA: A Benchmark for Health Reasoning over Real-World Wearable Data

Ji Soo Lee, Xilun Chen, Pierce Chuang et al.. WearableQA: A Benchmark for Health Reasoning over Real-World Wearable Data. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper introduces WearableQA, a benchmark designed to evaluate whether AI systems can reason over longitudinal wearable health data from real users. The benchmark consists of thousands of multiple-choice questions derived from wearable time series, blood biomarkers, and demographic information spanning hundreds of days per user, preserving authentic signal characteristics such as device noise and inter-individual variability. Questions are organized across two reasoning axes—data versus health reasoning, and single- versus cross-signal reasoning—and are constructed using a dual-grounding framework that anchors questions in both published physiological literature and statistically validated population-level patterns. Evaluation of a diverse set of proprietary and open-source large language models reveals substantial performance variation, with most models falling well below ceiling performance, indicating that the benchmark effectively differentiates model capabilities. The authors conclude that WearableQA represents a realistic and diagnostically useful benchmark for assessing LLM reasoning over real-world wearable health data, with considerable room remaining for improvement.

Parametric and feedback-controlled multiparameter quantum estimation in a double cavity optomechanics: steady and dynamical state

Hamza Harraf, Mohamed Amazioug, Amjad Sohail et al.. Parametric and feedback-controlled multiparameter quantum estimation in a double cavity optomechanics: steady and dynamical state. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper investigates the fundamental limits of simultaneously estimating multiple parameters in an open optomechanical system, where dissipation, thermal noise, and measurement incompatibility pose key challenges. The authors study a coupled-cavity platform featuring two mechanical modes interacting with driven optical cavities, two-mode squeezed vacuum, intracavity degenerate parametric amplification, and coherent optical feedback, using the continuous-variable Gaussian-state formalism to derive the linearized quantum Langevin dynamics and steady-state covariance matrix. Quantum Fisher information matrices for simultaneous estimation of the optomechanical coupling strength and cavity dissipation rate are evaluated using both symmetric and right logarithmic derivative frameworks. They find that intracavity parametric amplification substantially improves multiparameter estimation precision across a broad range of operating conditions, while coherent feedback produces a nonmonotonic effect on estimation precision that depends sensitively on feedback reflectivity, phase, squeezing strength, and thermal occupation. This nonmonotonic behavior reframes coherent feedback not as a simple enhancing or degrading tool but as a tunable resource for engineering quantum fluctuations and parameter-dependent correlations, with squeezing and parametric amplification together providing the greatest metrological gains in identifiable parameter regimes.

MEOX: Compact Multimodal Mixture-of-Experts for Earth Observation

Mohanad Albughdadi. MEOX: Compact Multimodal Mixture-of-Experts for Earth Observation. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper introduces MEOX, a compact multimodal masked autoencoder designed for Earth observation that can handle heterogeneous remote sensing sensors and missing data without requiring a large architecture. The model uses sensor-specific adapters, sparse mixture-of-experts blocks with shared and private low-rank components, and metadata tokens to achieve flexible multimodal fusion while constraining parameter growth. It was pretrained on a large collection of multimodal Earth observation samples using modality-balanced masked reconstruction and structured sensor dropout, then evaluated through frozen and fine-tuned transfer on multiple benchmark tasks including land cover segmentation and scene classification. MEOX achieves competitive or superior performance compared to larger existing models on several benchmarks, including outperforming a previously reported mixture-of-experts baseline on cashew segmentation and EuroSAT classification tasks. Diagnostic analyses further characterize how experts specialize by modality, spatial region, and function, while probing experiments quantify the contribution of geographic metadata to representation quality.

Does Your Agent's Memory Survive a Model Upgrade? A Controlled Study of Memory Portability

Ankit Goyal, Jaideep Ray. Does Your Agent's Memory Survive a Model Upgrade? A Controlled Study of Memory Portability. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper investigates whether AI agent memory systems remain functional and accurate after a model upgrade, a scenario that is common in practice but rarely studied systematically. The researchers compared four memory storage strategies—verbatim long-context history, retrieval-augmented generation with chunked text, model-compressed natural-language notes, and a fixed-schema knowledge graph—by testing how well each format transfers when the underlying language model is swapped. Using synthetic agent histories with randomized answer codes and two open-weight small language models, they evaluated retrieval accuracy before and after model migration under controlled conditions. They found that fixed-schema knowledge graphs transfer with negligible accuracy loss, whereas compressed notes are highly coupled to the model that wrote them, showing asymmetric accuracy shifts depending on migration direction, and that partial embedding index migrations recover only a fraction of the performance gains achievable through full re-embedding. The study concludes that retaining original source histories is critical for memory repair after upgrades, and that migration testing must account for directionality, embedding space isolation, and the distinct failure modes of each memory format.

A Log-Free $n^{1/5}$ Bound for Chowla's Cosine Problem

Abhishek Shankar. A Log-Free $n^{1/5}$ Bound for Chowla's Cosine Problem. arXiv. 2026 preprint

The paper addresses Chowla's cosine problem, which asks how large the minimum negative value of a trigonometric sum over a finite set of positive integers must be. A recent result had established a lower bound of the form the set size to the power of one-fifth, but with a subpolynomial loss in the exponent. This work eliminates that loss entirely, proving a clean lower bound with an absolute positive constant and no logarithmic penalty. The key technical innovation is an exact averaging identity for the asymmetric boundaries of additive intersections, which replaces a multiplicative-amplification step in the prior argument that was responsible for the logarithmic degradation. The result thus achieves a log-free version of the bound while otherwise building on the structure of the earlier proof.

Bedside identification of subphenotypes in acute respiratory failure (PHIND): a multicentre, observational cohort study

Kiran Reddy, Pratik Sinha, David Antcliffe et al.. Bedside identification of subphenotypes in acute respiratory failure (PHIND): a multicentre, observational cohort study. The Lancet Respiratory Medicine. 2026

This prospective multicentre observational study investigated whether inflammatory subphenotypes of acute respiratory distress syndrome (ARDS) could be identified rapidly at the bedside and whether they were associated with differing clinical outcomes. Using a near-patient immunoanalyser measuring plasma interleukin-6 and soluble TNF receptor-1, combined with plasma bicarbonate and a validated logistic regression model, patients with ARDS or acute hypoxaemic respiratory failure were classified within approximately one hour of sample collection as either hyperinflammatory or hypoinflammatory. The study enrolled several hundred patients across intensive care units in the UK and Ireland, finding that a minority were classified as hyperinflammatory while the majority were hypoinflammatory. Sixty-day mortality was substantially higher in the hyperinflammatory subphenotype, with adjusted analyses confirming significantly increased odds of death independent of other factors. The authors conclude that bedside subphenotyping of ARDS is feasible within a clinically actionable timeframe and that the findings support precision medicine approaches and the need for prospective, subphenotype-stratified interventional trials.

Diagnostic Accuracy of Immature Granulocyte Count for Early Sepsis Detection: A Comparative Evaluation With C-Reactive Protein and Procalcitonin.

Taver R, Gupta V, Saxena P et al.. Diagnostic Accuracy of Immature Granulocyte Count for Early Sepsis Detection: A Comparative Evaluation With C-Reactive Protein and Procalcitonin.. Cureus. 2026

This study investigated whether immature granulocyte parameters, specifically immature granulocyte count and percentage derived from routine complete blood counts, could serve as early diagnostic biomarkers for sepsis in critically ill patients, comparing their accuracy against the established markers C-reactive protein and procalcitonin. In a retrospective observational design, critically ill intensive care unit patients were categorized into sepsis and non-sepsis groups based on clinical monitoring within the first day of admission, and diagnostic performance was evaluated using receiver operating characteristic curve analysis. Patients who developed sepsis showed significantly elevated immature granulocyte parameters alongside other inflammatory and organ dysfunction markers compared to those who did not. Immature granulocyte percentage demonstrated good diagnostic accuracy, outperforming C-reactive protein and performing comparably to procalcitonin, while the combination of immature granulocyte percentage with procalcitonin achieved the strongest overall discriminatory performance. The authors conclude that immature granulocyte parameters, being readily obtainable from standard hematological analyses, represent reliable early indicators of sepsis and could support timely clinical decision-making when incorporated into routine assessment.

Mechanism-informed diagnostic accuracy of blood biomarkers for sepsis-associated encephalopathy: a systematic review and Bayesian diagnostic network meta-analysis.

Zhang Q, Zhu R, Li Y et al.. Mechanism-informed diagnostic accuracy of blood biomarkers for sepsis-associated encephalopathy: a systematic review and Bayesian diagnostic network meta-analysis.. Frontiers in molecular neuroscience. 2026

This systematic review investigated the diagnostic accuracy of blood biomarkers for sepsis-associated encephalopathy, a serious form of acute brain dysfunction occurring in sepsis patients, by examining candidates spanning innate immune activation, blood-brain barrier disruption, glial response, and neuronal injury. The researchers conducted a PRISMA-compliant systematic review and Bayesian diagnostic network meta-analysis across multiple databases, pooling studies with extractable diagnostic data and characterizing heterogeneity according to how SAE was defined and when biomarkers were sampled relative to a clinical anchor event. Blood-brain barrier and glial markers such as GFAP and S100β generally demonstrated more balanced diagnostic discrimination than classic neuronal injury markers, while upstream immune and alarmin candidates ranked highly in Bayesian comparative analyses, though several top-ranked biomarkers were supported by only single studies. A key limitation identified was that few studies assessed whether biomarkers provide diagnostic information beyond established clinical predictors such as illness severity scores or sedation-related confounders, restricting immediate clinical applicability. The authors conclude that blood biomarkers for this condition reflect a biologically coherent immune-to-neuronal cascade but that prospective head-to-head validation under standardized phenotyping and sampling protocols is needed to establish their incremental diagnostic value.

Plasma and urinary sTREM-1 as biomarkers of sepsis-associated acute kidney injury in critically Ill children.

Magdy SM, El-Gawaad TA, Hussein FA et al.. Plasma and urinary sTREM-1 as biomarkers of sepsis-associated acute kidney injury in critically Ill children.. European journal of pediatrics. 2026

This prospective observational cohort study investigated whether plasma and urinary levels of soluble triggering receptor expressed on myeloid cells-1 (sTREM-1) could serve as early biomarkers for sepsis-associated acute kidney injury in critically ill children admitted to a pediatric intensive care unit. Researchers measured sTREM-1 levels at the time of sepsis diagnosis and again at the point of acute kidney injury diagnosis in those who developed the complication, using established pediatric scoring criteria to define both sepsis and kidney injury. Both plasma and urinary sTREM-1 concentrations were significantly elevated in children who subsequently developed sepsis-associated acute kidney injury compared to those who did not, and both biomarkers rose further following acute kidney injury onset. Receiver operating characteristic analyses demonstrated moderate discriminative ability for both plasma and urinary sTREM-1, with plasma levels showing somewhat better diagnostic performance. The authors conclude that sTREM-1 measured at sepsis diagnosis may support early risk stratification, and that urinary sTREM-1 additionally showed prognostic value for mortality in affected children.

Diagnostic value of cystatin C in acute kidney injury among patients with sepsis: a systematic review and meta-analysis.

Cheng L, Zhang B, Hu X et al.. Diagnostic value of cystatin C in acute kidney injury among patients with sepsis: a systematic review and meta-analysis.. Frontiers in medicine. 2026

This systematic review and meta-analysis evaluated the diagnostic utility of cystatin C for early detection of acute kidney injury in patients with sepsis. Researchers systematically searched electronic databases for relevant studies, assessed their quality using the QUADAS-2 tool, and applied a random-effects meta-analysis alongside summary receiver operating characteristic curve analysis to pool diagnostic accuracy estimates. Across the included studies, cystatin C demonstrated good overall diagnostic performance for sepsis-associated acute kidney injury, with subgroup analyses showing that studies applying Sepsis 3.0 diagnostic criteria yielded higher specificity and reduced heterogeneity. Combining cystatin C with other biomarkers further improved diagnostic accuracy. The authors conclude that cystatin C is a promising biomarker for early clinical identification of sepsis-associated acute kidney injury, though heterogeneity among included studies warrants cautious interpretation.

Surviving Sepsis Campaign: International Guidelines for Management of Sepsis and Septic Shock 2026

Hallie C. Prescott, Massimo Antonelli, Waleed Alhazzani et al.. Surviving Sepsis Campaign: International Guidelines for Management of Sepsis and Septic Shock 2026. Critical Care Medicine. 2026

This paper presents the 2026 Surviving Sepsis Campaign international guidelines for the management of sepsis and septic shock in adults. The guidelines were developed by a large, geographically diverse, multidisciplinary committee using the GRADE methodology, incorporating evidence synthesis, patient and family input, and an Evidence to Decision framework to formulate recommendations across domains including screening, infection management, hemodynamics, respiratory support, adjunctive therapies, and goals of care. The guidelines address management across hospital, prehospital, and immediate post-hospital settings, with an emphasis on antimicrobial stewardship, early identification, and timely treatment. Recommendations range from strong to conditional depending on the certainty of evidence and balance of desirable versus undesirable effects, with particular attention to applicability in low- and middle-income country contexts. The guidelines aim to provide actionable, evidence-based guidance to support clinicians and health systems in improving outcomes for the tens of millions of patients affected by sepsis globally each year.

A systematic literature review on the effects of exercise on human Toll-like receptor expression

Kasper Favere, Matthias Bosman, Peter Delputte et al.. A systematic literature review on the effects of exercise on human Toll-like receptor expression. PubMed. 2026

This systematic review examined how different forms of exercise affect the expression of Toll-like receptors (TLRs), a family of immune receptors involved in triggering inflammatory responses that have been implicated in chronic low-grade inflammation associated with sedentary lifestyles and non-communicable diseases. The authors searched multiple databases for human studies in which exercise was the sole intervention, ultimately including studies spanning acute and chronic resistance, aerobic, and combined training modalities. Acute resistance exercise tended to upregulate TLR expression, whereas acute aerobic exercise generally produced neutral or downregulatory effects, particularly in healthy individuals. Chronic resistance and combined training programs were predominantly associated with unchanged or decreased TLR levels, while chronic aerobic exercise produced mixed results with most measurements showing no change in expression. The authors conclude that exercise does interact with TLR signalling in a manner dependent on exercise type and duration, but substantial heterogeneity across study designs limits firm conclusions and underscores the need for further standardized research.

Explainable AI in healthcare: a systematic review of XAI use cases in imaging, diagnostics, and rehabilitation

Apoorva Aravindkumar, Marimuthu Ramadoss, Saqhibuddeen Ahmed Fakhruddin Ahmed et al.. Explainable AI in healthcare: a systematic review of XAI use cases in imaging, diagnostics, and rehabilitation. Frontiers in Artificial Intelligence. 2026

This systematic review examined how explainable artificial intelligence methods are being applied across healthcare domains including medical imaging, diagnostics, and rehabilitation, with the goal of clarifying which XAI techniques are used, how they pair with different data types and models, and how they support clinical understanding. The authors followed established reporting guidelines to screen a large body of literature, ultimately analyzing several dozen studies identified through multiple academic databases. They found that a clear method-modality fit has emerged in practice, with saliency and heat-map approaches such as Grad-CAM favored for spatial interpretation in imaging tasks, while feature-attribution tools such as SHAP and LIME dominate in diagnostic and rehabilitation contexts where global and case-level explanations are needed. Many studies combined two or more XAI methods to cross-validate interpretations, reflecting a trend toward more robust explanatory frameworks. The authors conclude that while current healthcare XAI practice is maturing, trustworthy clinical deployment will require standardized reporting practices, rigorous evaluation of explanation faithfulness and stability, and external validation across diverse sites.
