Point prevalence assessments of five respiratory viruses in adults ≥ 50 years of age in Germany by multi-pathogen rapid antigen testing on June 1, 2024, and December 15, 2024.

Anggitakirana M, Nacov JA, Salmanton-García J et al.. Point prevalence assessments of five respiratory viruses in adults ≥ 50 years of age in Germany by multi-pathogen rapid antigen testing on June 1, 2024, and December 15, 2024.. Infection. 2026

This study investigated the circulation of five respiratory viruses among community-dwelling older adults in Germany using two point-prevalence assessments conducted in summer and winter. Participants aged fifty years and older performed multi-pathogen rapid antigen self-tests capable of detecting adenovirus, SARS-CoV-2, influenza A and B, and respiratory syncytial virus, while also reporting symptoms, healthcare use, and work absenteeism. The overall pathogen detection rate was low, with the majority of tests returning negative results, yet adenovirus was the most frequently detected pathogen in both assessment rounds, a finding underrepresented in routine surveillance systems. SARS-CoV-2 was associated with the greatest clinical burden, most often leading to healthcare consultations and work absenteeism. The authors conclude that community-based point-prevalence screening can uncover asymptomatic reservoirs and underreported viral activity, and that adenovirus warrants closer attention as a potentially common co-infecting agent in older adults.

'No, it is a virus, your body will fight it.' The value of diagnostic testing for managing respiratory viruses in primary care: an observational study from a post-COVID pilot programme.

Wesley-Smith L, Dowell A, Turner N et al.. 'No, it is a virus, your body will fight it.' The value of diagnostic testing for managing respiratory viruses in primary care: an observational study from a post-COVID pilot programme.. Journal of primary health care. 2026

This observational study examined how laboratory-based polymerase chain reaction testing for respiratory viruses, combined with rapid COVID-19 antigen testing, influenced patient management in primary care during the pandemic. Researchers reviewed electronic medical records from a single urban general practice in New Zealand, analyzing data on test results, antibiotic prescribing, patient presentations, and follow-up communications over a period spanning ninety days before and after testing. Roughly half of presentations yielded a positive polymerase chain reaction result, yet antibiotics were prescribed for more than a third of all presentations, and notably, patients who tested positive for a respiratory virus were more likely to receive antibiotics than those who tested negative. While test results were sometimes used effectively for follow-up via text and email, the study identified gaps in result communication and considerable variation in prescribing behaviour following test results. The authors conclude that environmental, clinical, and psychological factors beyond testing limitations shape how results are applied, and suggest that faster point-of-care technologies may partially but not fully resolve these issues.

Respiratory multi-viral rapid-antigen point-of-care tests in New Zealand primary care: a mixed-methods pilot.

Bibby S, Marsh S, Dowell A et al.. Respiratory multi-viral rapid-antigen point-of-care tests in New Zealand primary care: a mixed-methods pilot.. BJGP open. 2026

This study examined the feasibility and acceptability of integrating multi-viral rapid-antigen point-of-care tests into routine primary care for patients presenting with acute respiratory symptoms. Using a prospective mixed-methods design across seven primary care clinics during a winter period, researchers collected swab results alongside data from electronic medical records, clinician and patient surveys, and qualitative interviews and focus groups. The tests were well-received by both clinicians and patients, with all clinicians finding the tests useful and incorporating them into practice, and the majority of patients expressing a desire for the tests to be available in future. A substantial proportion of prescribers reported that test results influenced their antibiotic prescribing decisions, and the tests also informed some secondary care referral choices. The authors conclude that routine integration of these multi-viral tests into primary care could improve the management of common respiratory illnesses and encourage patient behaviours that reduce transmission.

Evaluation of the QuickFinder COVID-19/Flu/RSV antigen test for rapid simultaneous detection of SARS-CoV-2, influenza A/B viruses, and respiratory syncytial virus.

Kim JA, Kang M, Yu HJ et al.. Evaluation of the QuickFinder COVID-19/Flu/RSV antigen test for rapid simultaneous detection of SARS-CoV-2, influenza A/B viruses, and respiratory syncytial virus.. Diagnostic microbiology and infectious disease. 2026

This study evaluated the diagnostic performance of the QuickFinder COVID-19/Flu/RSV Antigen Test, a lateral flow immunoassay designed for simultaneous rapid detection of SARS-CoV-2, influenza A and B, and respiratory syncytial virus. Archived nasopharyngeal swab specimens were tested in parallel with the QuickFinder and real-time reverse transcription polymerase chain reaction, which served as the reference standard, and cross-reactivity was assessed against a panel of common respiratory pathogens. The test demonstrated high sensitivity for SARS-CoV-2 and influenza A, with somewhat lower sensitivity for influenza B and RSV, while achieving perfect specificity for all four targets and showing no cross-reactivity with any of the tested microorganisms. Sensitivity was consistently strong in specimens with high viral loads but declined in low viral load samples. The authors conclude that the QuickFinder is a reliable rapid screening and differentiation tool for high-viral-load specimens but should complement rather than replace molecular testing due to its reduced sensitivity at lower viral loads.

No detection of respiratory syncytial virus at one clinic in Japan with rapid immunochromatographic antigen tests for three viruses using the same nasopharyngeal swabs from adult patients

Kajiwara N, Tsunetoshi Y, Shiode S. No detection of respiratory syncytial virus at one clinic in Japan with rapid immunochromatographic antigen tests for three viruses using the same nasopharyngeal swabs from adult patients. 2026

This study investigated the disease burden of respiratory syncytial virus (RSV) infection among adult patients in Japan by testing nasopharyngeal swabs collected from adults presenting with acute respiratory infection symptoms at a single clinic. Samples were analyzed using rapid immunochromatographic antigen tests capable of simultaneously detecting SARS-CoV-2, influenza A and B viruses, and RSV. While positive cases were identified for SARS-CoV-2 and influenza viruses, no RSV-positive cases were detected among the patients tested. Comparison with prior studies that employed polymerase chain reaction testing revealed a statistically significant difference in RSV detection rates, with PCR identifying substantially more positive cases. The authors conclude that rapid immunochromatographic antigen testing has limited diagnostic utility for RSV infections in Japanese adults compared to PCR-based approaches.

Can exercise affect immune function to increase susceptibility to infection?

Richard J. Simpson, Richard J. Simpson, Maree Gleeson et al.. Can exercise affect immune function to increase susceptibility to infection?. PubMed. 2026

This debate article examines the contested question of whether exercise itself can suppress immune function and increase susceptibility to infection. The paper contrasts the broadly accepted view that regular moderate-intensity exercise benefits immune defense with the observation that high-performance athletes and military personnel experience elevated infection burdens, which has historically been attributed to arduous training. Opposing groups debated four key issues: whether athletes are genuinely more infection-prone than the general population, whether exercise independently alters immunity beyond the effects of confounding factors such as sleep disruption, stress, travel, and nutritional deficits, whether commonly used biomarkers and in vitro data adequately capture immune health in this context, and whether the existing scientific evidence is sufficient to support claims about exercise-induced immune suppression. Both sides agreed that infection susceptibility is multifactorial in nature, but the central question of whether exercise per se causally increases infection risk in athletes remains unresolved, and the authors call for more rigorous empirical research to address it.

Spinal cord injury: Known and possible influences on the immune response to exercise

Christof A. Leicht, Victoria L. Goosey‐Tolfrey, Nicolette C. Bishop. Spinal cord injury: Known and possible influences on the immune response to exercise. PubMed. 2026

This review paper examined how spinal cord injury affects immune function, with a particular focus on how exercise influences immune responses in this population. The authors conducted a comprehensive literature search to gather existing research on exercise immunology across patient, recreationally active, and athlete populations with spinal cord injury. The paper explains that autonomic dysfunction following spinal cord injury, especially in individuals with tetraplegia, alters catecholamine concentrations at rest and after exercise, which likely depresses immunity given that most lymphoid cells express adrenergic receptors, though other contributing factors such as altered muscular, endocrine, and cardiovascular function were also considered. The authors found that while some immune impairments result from these neuroendocrine disruptions, other responses — such as increased natural killer cell cytotoxicity and mucosal immunity responses to exercise in tetraplegia — appear to operate through different or compensatory mechanisms, including sympathetic reflex activity, receptor hypersensitivity, and parasympathetic adjustments. The review concludes that exercise and rehabilitation can improve certain immune and autonomic measures in individuals with spinal cord injury, suggesting that exercise may serve as a practical tool for maintaining or improving health in this population, though further research is needed to clarify the underlying mechanisms.

Disorders of Gut–Brain Interaction and the Rome V Process

Douglas A. Drossman, L Chang, Jan Tack. Disorders of Gut–Brain Interaction and the Rome V Process. Gastroenterology. 2026

This paper describes the development and scope of the Rome V criteria for disorders of gut–brain interaction, which are gastrointestinal conditions arising from disturbances in motility, visceral sensitivity, mucosal function, gut microbiota, or central nervous system processing. The Rome V process involved a comprehensive review of evidence accumulated since the previous Rome IV criteria were published, aiming to refine and update the diagnostic and therapeutic framework for these conditions. Key updates include revised disorder definitions, new information on the roles of diet, the gut microenvironment, pharmacogenomics, and biopsychosocial and cross-cultural factors, as well as efforts to eliminate imprecise or stigmatizing terminology. The process also produced updated diagnostic algorithms and incorporated patient illness experiences and sociocultural perspectives into clinical guidance. Overall, Rome V represents an evidence-based advancement in the classification and management of gut–brain interaction disorders.

Understanding graft-versus-host disease. Preliminary findings regarding the effects of exercise in affected patients

Carmen Fiuza‐Luces, Nuria Garatachea, Nathan A. Berger et al.. Understanding graft-versus-host disease. Preliminary findings regarding the effects of exercise in affected patients. PubMed. 2026

This paper examines graft-versus-host disease, a potentially multi-systemic complication that arises when donor lymphocytes attack host tissues following allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplantation, with particular attention to its chronic form and its impact on physical capacity. The authors reviewed the pathophysiological mechanisms underlying the disease alongside available evidence from both animal and human studies on the effects of exercise in affected patients. Their review suggests that exercise training as an adjunct therapy holds promise for improving physical fitness, potentially enhancing immune function, and reducing fatigue in this population. However, they conclude that robust evidence remains limited and that randomized controlled trials focused specifically on patients with graft-versus-host disease are needed to establish firmer recommendations.

New approach methodologies for drug discovery

Wenqiang Liu, Paul Pang, Catherine A. Wu et al.. New approach methodologies for drug discovery. Cell. 2026

This review examines the growing field of new approach methodologies as alternatives to traditional animal-based drug discovery, which suffers from high failure rates in translating findings to humans. The authors survey recent advances in stem cell-based, organoid-based, and computational approaches, showing how these human-centered tools now cover the full drug-discovery pipeline from disease modeling and drug design through efficacy testing. The review also highlights the emerging potential of these methodologies not only as research tools but as therapeutic treatments in their own right, discussed in both preclinical and clinical contexts. Key biological, technical, and regulatory obstacles to broader adoption are identified, including challenges related to model fidelity and evolving oversight frameworks from major regulatory and funding bodies. The authors conclude by outlining translational and societal considerations needed for responsible implementation and describing future human-centric pipelines that could fundamentally reshape drug discovery.

Oxidative Stress in Health and Disease: Mechanisms and Therapeutic Perspectives

Shehwaz Anwar, Hajed Obaid A. Alharbi, Ali Yousif Babiker et al.. Oxidative Stress in Health and Disease: Mechanisms and Therapeutic Perspectives. International Journal of Molecular Sciences. 2026

This review examines the dual role of reactive oxygen species in biological systems, where they serve as essential signaling molecules at physiological levels but drive disease when produced in excess or dysregulated. The authors synthesize current knowledge on the major types, cellular sources, and molecular mechanisms of reactive oxygen species, including their involvement in redox signaling and oxidative damage pathways. The review evaluates contemporary and emerging methods for detecting and quantifying oxidative stress in clinical and preclinical settings, encompassing biochemical assays, fluorescent probes, biosensors, and advanced imaging approaches. The contribution of oxidative stress to major diseases—including cancer, diabetes, cardiovascular disorders, neurodegeneration, and inflammatory conditions—is examined in the context of these mechanisms. The authors conclude by outlining future directions in precision redox medicine, emphasizing targeted antioxidant-based diagnostic and therapeutic strategies informed by improved detection technologies and deeper understanding of redox biology.

UniMate: One Unified Model to Animate Diverse Skeletons

Linzhan Mou, Jiahui Lei, Zhiyang Dou et al.. UniMate: One Unified Model to Animate Diverse Skeletons. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper addresses the challenge of generating motion for arbitrary rigged 3D characters, a task where existing methods are constrained by fixed skeleton topologies or require per-skeleton fine-tuning and reference motions at test time. The authors developed UniMate, a unified foundation model that takes a rigged 3D asset and a text prompt as inputs and synthesizes articulated motion without any test-time optimization or retraining. The model employs a topology-aware diffusion transformer that encodes skeletal structure through graph-aware attention biases derived from pairwise joint relations and geodesic distances, a spectral rotary position embedding generalized to arbitrary kinematic trees via the graph Laplacian, and a global topological conditioner. To train the model, the authors curated a large-scale dataset spanning a wide variety of creature and object categories with unified canonicalization and text pairing. UniMate is reported to outperform existing baselines in quality, generalization, and efficiency, while also enabling zero-shot cross-topology transfer, motion in-betweening, sequence expansion, and text-guided editing.

Two extremely irradiated volatile-rich sub-Neptunes with companions in the TOI-426 and TOI-1839 systems: Insights into arrival and survival near the lower edge of the Neptunian desert

A. Castro-González, O. Barragán, D. J. Armstrong et al.. Two extremely irradiated volatile-rich sub-Neptunes with companions in the TOI-426 and TOI-1839 systems: Insights into arrival and survival near the lower edge of the Neptunian desert. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper confirms two highly irradiated sub-Neptune planets near the lower boundary of the Neptunian desert—a region of parameter space where close-in planets with intermediate sizes and masses are rarely found—using space-based photometry and ground-based radial velocity measurements. The study also detects additional companions in both systems, including a long-period giant planet and a transiting sub-Neptune. Despite receiving extreme stellar irradiation, both confirmed planets retain substantial volatile content, and atmospheric escape modeling strongly favors water-dominated rather than hydrogen/helium envelopes, pointing to a steam-world composition with atmospheric loss timescales far exceeding the ages of typical planetary systems. Interior structure models thus challenge the idea that the desert's lower edge is defined purely by photoevaporative stripping of primordial envelopes. A population-level analysis further reveals a statistically significant excess of outer giant companions around sub-Neptunes located within the tidal survival band near the desert edge, supporting the hypothesis that high-eccentricity tidal migration plays a major role in delivering and sculpting planets in this region.

Necessary or Sufficient? Evaluating LLM Explanations With Behavioural Evidence

Urja Pawar, Rajitha Ramanayake, Nabeel Kemal et al.. Necessary or Sufficient? Evaluating LLM Explanations With Behavioural Evidence. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper investigates whether explanations produced by large language models alongside their decisions accurately reflect the factors that actually drive those decisions. The researchers tested two formal interpretations of explanatory factors: necessity, meaning that altering a named factor changes the model's output, and sufficiency, meaning that retaining a named factor while removing other information preserves the output. Using controlled black-box interventions across two synthetic tasks — recommending advisors to clients and assessing prompts for harmfulness — the study evaluated eight models from three major model families. They found moderate correlations between the factors models cited as most influential and the measured necessity and sufficiency scores, and in a substantial proportion of cases an uncited factor demonstrated stronger measured influence than the lowest-ranked cited factor. The authors conclude that while model-provided explanations carry useful signal, they do not reliably identify the true top drivers of individual decisions, raising concerns for their use in agent oversight and error diagnosis.

Molecular Déjà Vu: Digit-Level Retrieval of Published Values in Frontier Language Models

Matthias Busch, Marius Tacke, Sviatlana V. Lamaka et al.. Molecular Déjà Vu: Digit-Level Retrieval of Published Values in Frontier Language Models. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper investigates whether large language models genuinely predict molecular properties or simply retrieve published numerical values memorized from training data. The authors audited a large set of frontier language models across multiple molecular regression benchmarks, testing for verbatim digit-level retrieval of known property values. They found that verbatim retrieval is widespread and benchmark-specific, with the majority of models showing clear retrieval behavior on roughly half the datasets tested, and that higher reasoning levels substantially increase the rate at which retrieval is detected. Attempts to disrupt retrieval by transforming molecular representations showed that the strongest models could still recognize transformed inputs paired with original labels, and suppressing retrieval brought model prediction errors closer together, suggesting that memorization—rather than genuine predictive capability—is a major driver of apparent performance differences between models. The work concludes that current molecular property benchmarks cannot reliably distinguish true property prediction from data memorization in large language models.

Propagation Model for SSC attacks: Why SBOM (tools) don't tell the whole truth

Ljubica Grgic, Lazar Maksimovic, Pavel Laskov. Propagation Model for SSC attacks: Why SBOM (tools) don't tell the whole truth. arXiv. 2026 preprint

The paper investigates the limitations of Software Bill of Materials (SBOM) tools in securing software supply chains, arguing that these tools fail to adequately capture how vulnerabilities propagate through a chain and affect exploitability. To address this gap, the authors propose a four-stage propagation model centered on how attack effects spread, and empirically evaluate several open-source SBOM tools against each stage using real-world open-source projects and the Log4j vulnerability as a test case. The findings reveal that existing SBOM tools consistently address only the first two stages—structural exposure and vulnerability class presence—while lacking the capabilities needed for code reachability analysis and taint path analysis, which constitute the deeper stages of propagation. The authors argue that this gap means current tooling cannot adequately explain or predict how vulnerabilities evolve into systemic cyber risks across supply chains. They conclude by calling for propagation effects to be placed at the center of future software supply chain security research and tool design.

Full-field laser-Doppler-vibrometry FRF dataset for a four-bolt aluminum plate under bolt-torque variation

Berkay Kullukcu, Dina Hannebauer. Full-field laser-Doppler-vibrometry FRF dataset for a four-bolt aluminum plate under bolt-torque variation. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper describes a dataset created from laser Doppler vibrometry experiments on an aluminum plate fastened with four bolts, designed to capture how vibration characteristics change with bolt tightening torque. The experimental setup included eighteen distinct torque states, ranging from all bolts fully tightened to all bolts loose, as well as intermediate combinations involving single or multiple loose bolts. Measurements were taken across a wide frequency range at fine resolution, with data collected at dozens of scan points for every torque state. The resulting dataset includes amplitude and phase files, resonance frequency tables, frequency response function correlation values, and supporting documentation and code. This resource is intended to support research into structural health monitoring, bolted joint dynamics, and related areas requiring well-characterized vibration data under controlled joint condition variability.

Advances in Pathogen Detection by Nanosensors: Biorecognition Strategies, Signal Amplification, and Platform Engineering

Aroofa Zargul, He Liu, Weiyu Zhang et al.. Advances in Pathogen Detection by Nanosensors: Biorecognition Strategies, Signal Amplification, and Platform Engineering. ACS Nano. 2026

This review examines the current state and future directions of nanosensor-based technologies for detecting pathogens in the context of infectious disease and antimicrobial resistance. The authors identify critical limitations of conventional diagnostic methods, including culture, ELISA, and microscopy, such as slow turnaround times and poor sensitivity for low-abundance targets. The review systematically evaluates three converging domains: biorecognition elements that enable selective pathogen capture in complex biological samples, signal amplification strategies that push detection sensitivity to femtomolar and single-cell levels, and integrated platform engineering combining CRISPR-Cas systems, artificial intelligence, and microfluidics for point-of-care deployment. Performance is assessed across standardized metrics to highlight both opportunities for synergy and persistent barriers to clinical translation. The authors argue that these combined advances define a strategic framework for next-generation diagnostics capable of transforming infectious disease surveillance and guiding therapeutic decision-making.

Advances in tuberculosis diagnostics and biomarkers, 2020-26: scientific evidence, recommendations, gaps, and developmental priorities.

Walzl G, McHugh TD, Chakaya J et al.. Advances in tuberculosis diagnostics and biomarkers, 2020-26: scientific evidence, recommendations, gaps, and developmental priorities.. The Lancet. Microbe. 2027

This review synthesizes major advances in tuberculosis diagnostics and biomarker research over a five-year period, covering how new evidence has shaped updated WHO policy recommendations that consolidate detection of tuberculosis disease and drug resistance into a unified framework. Digital chest radiography with computer-aided detection has been elevated to a core screening and triage tool, while decentralised molecular diagnosis has expanded through multiple classes of rapid nucleic acid amplification tests deployed at varying levels of the health system. Novel developments include near point-of-care molecular platforms, non-sputum specimen types such as tongue swabs and stool, and programmatic strategies like sputum pooling, all of which have improved bacteriological confirmation particularly in children and others unable to produce sputum. Drug-resistance testing has advanced through dedicated rapid molecular assays and targeted next-generation sequencing at reference laboratory level, while urine-based lipoarabinomannan assays provide diagnostic benefit in people with HIV and advanced disease. Despite these gains, the review identifies persistent performance, implementation, and equity gaps, and notes that although host blood transcriptomic and other biomarker signatures show promising prognostic utility, none yet meets WHO criteria for a point-of-care test capable of reliably distinguishing infection from disease, including subclinical tuberculosis.

Molecular Diagnostics of Infectious Diseases.

Takata M, Chikumi H, Yoshifuji A. Molecular Diagnostics of Infectious Diseases.. Yonago acta medica. 2026

This review examines the current state and future directions of molecular diagnostic methods applied to infectious diseases. The paper covers two principal approaches: nucleic acid amplification techniques for detecting pathogen genetic material, and next-generation sequencing for characterizing pathogen properties, identifying novel agents, and tracking outbreak transmission. Amplification methods discussed include polymerase chain reaction and its variants optimized for speed, quantification, and point-of-care use, as well as isothermal amplification techniques suited to settings without sophisticated thermal cycling equipment, and emerging CRISPR-based detection strategies. Next-generation sequencing is highlighted for its ability to rapidly generate large volumes of genomic data, enabling diagnosis of complex infections, surveillance of pathogen evolution, and microbiome analysis relevant to disease susceptibility. The authors conclude that future advances in molecular diagnostics will come from integrating these complementary technologies into hybrid platforms that address the current limitations of individual methods.

SARS-CoV-2 Point-of-Care Testing Modalities: Integrating Molecular, Immunological, Biosensor, and AI Approaches.

Hetta HF, Ahmed R, Haseeb A et al.. SARS-CoV-2 Point-of-Care Testing Modalities: Integrating Molecular, Immunological, Biosensor, and AI Approaches.. Diagnostics (Basel, Switzerland). 2026

This review examines the landscape of point-of-care testing technologies developed for SARS-CoV-2 diagnosis, motivated by the urgent diagnostic demands exposed by the COVID-19 pandemic. The authors conducted a structured literature search across major databases and evaluated multiple testing modalities, including molecular assays such as rapid RT-PCR, LAMP, and CRISPR-based methods, alongside antigen and antibody tests, biosensor platforms, and artificial intelligence-assisted diagnostic approaches. Molecular methods were found to offer the highest sensitivity and specificity for decentralized use, while antigen tests provide rapid, affordable screening despite performance variability linked to viral load, symptom timing, and viral variants. Antibody-based testing proved more suited to seroprevalence and retrospective investigations than acute infection detection, and emerging biosensor and AI tools showed analytical promise but largely remain at prototype or early validation stages requiring further clinical evidence. The review concludes that no single modality is universally optimal, and that complementary use of these approaches, combined with advances in multiplexing, digital connectivity, and variant-resilient design, will be essential for COVID-19 management and broader infectious disease preparedness.

Clinical accuracy, performance, and usability of the Dragonfly point-of-care molecular diagnostic platform for mpox: a mixed-methods field evaluation in Uganda.

Bakamutumaho B, Baker K, Cavuto ML et al.. Clinical accuracy, performance, and usability of the Dragonfly point-of-care molecular diagnostic platform for mpox: a mixed-methods field evaluation in Uganda.. The Lancet. Infectious diseases. 2026

This study evaluated the clinical accuracy and usability of a point-of-care molecular diagnostic platform called Dragonfly for detecting mpox in Uganda, where centralized PCR testing creates logistical delays that hamper outbreak response. The researchers conducted a prospective diagnostic accuracy study across multiple health facilities in Kampala and Wakiso, enrolling individuals with suspected mpox who had active cutaneous lesions, and compared Dragonfly's results to confirmatory real-time quantitative PCR as the reference standard. Dragonfly uses a dual-target design to detect both orthopoxvirus and monkeypox virus, delivering results in under forty minutes. The platform demonstrated very high sensitivity and specificity for both targets across a broad range of viral loads, achieving overall agreement with reference PCR approaching unity. Front-line healthcare workers found the platform highly acceptable, particularly valuing the elimination of sample transport and centralized laboratory requirements, though they noted training and supply-chain considerations for routine deployment in resource-limited settings.

Concordance Between Fingerprick and Venous Whole Blood CD4 Counts Using the Visitect CD4 Advanced disease platform.

Adzemovic T, Nalintya E, Onencan P et al.. Concordance Between Fingerprick and Venous Whole Blood CD4 Counts Using the Visitect CD4 Advanced disease platform.. The Journal of infectious diseases. 2026

This study evaluated whether fingerprick blood samples could reliably substitute for venous whole blood when using the Visitect CD4 Advanced Disease platform, a rapid semi-quantitative assay that categorizes CD4 counts as above or below a clinically important threshold. The researchers conducted a prospective validation study directly comparing results obtained from fingerprick sampling against those from conventional venous whole blood collection using the same platform. They found a very high level of agreement between the two sampling methods, supporting the conclusion that fingerprick testing with this platform could serve as a reliable point-of-care diagnostic tool in resource-limited settings where conventional venous blood draw may not be feasible.

An integrated centrifugal microfluidic CRISPR-based diagnostics platform for multiplexed point-of-care testing of respiratory pathogens.

Li L, Wei H, Wei M et al.. An integrated centrifugal microfluidic CRISPR-based diagnostics platform for multiplexed point-of-care testing of respiratory pathogens.. Biosensors & bioelectronics. 2026

The paper developed and validated a point-of-care diagnostic platform called iCARD that combines centrifugal microfluidics with CRISPR-based detection to simultaneously identify multiple respiratory pathogens. The system uses a polymethyl methacrylate disc pre-loaded with lyophilized CRISPR/Cas13a reagents paired with a portable fluorescence analyzer, and incorporates a novel resin-based method to purify and concentrate nucleic acids from throat swabs. The platform can screen six respiratory pathogens across four independent samples in a single run within approximately forty minutes, achieving single-copy analytical sensitivity without cross-reactivity between targets. Clinical validation using retrospective patient throat swab samples demonstrated very high diagnostic sensitivity and perfect specificity, supporting its reliability for decentralized use. The authors conclude that iCARD offers a robust, high-throughput, and accurate solution for molecular diagnostics at the point of care and for epidemiological surveillance.

Surviving Sepsis Campaign: international guidelines for management of sepsis and septic shock 2026

Hallie C. Prescott, Massimo Antonelli, Waleed Alhazzanic et al.. Surviving Sepsis Campaign: international guidelines for management of sepsis and septic shock 2026. Intensive Care Medicine. 2026

The abstract provided is too thin to summarize meaningfully — it offers only background epidemiological context and a general statement about the importance of early identification and treatment, without describing the specific recommendations, methodology, or findings of the 2026 guidelines themselves.

EULAR recommendations for the management of rheumatoid arthritis with synthetic and biologic disease-modifying antirheumatic drugs: 2025 update

Josef S. Smolen, Christopher J. Edwards, Victoria Konzett et al.. EULAR recommendations for the management of rheumatoid arthritis with synthetic and biologic disease-modifying antirheumatic drugs: 2025 update. Annals of the Rheumatic Diseases. 2026

This paper presents the updated guidelines from a major European rheumatology alliance for managing rheumatoid arthritis using synthetic and biologic disease-modifying antirheumatic drugs. An international task force conducted systematic literature reviews on the safety and efficacy of these therapies and used a structured voting process to develop and refine recommendations. The resulting guidance consolidates overarching principles and a streamlined set of recommendations covering conventional synthetic agents, glucocorticoids, biologic therapies, and targeted synthetic drugs including Janus kinase inhibitors. The recommendations emphasize a treat-to-target strategy beginning with methotrexate and short-term glucocorticoids, escalating to biologic agents after insufficient response, and incorporating careful risk assessment for cardiovascular events, malignancies, and thromboembolic events before considering Janus kinase inhibitors. The guidelines also address treatment sequencing after failure of initial therapy and advise caution when tapering drugs following sustained remission, as discontinuation frequently leads to disease flare.

Smart wearable and implantable biosensors for continuous health monitoring: materials, biocompatibility, and AI integration

Thirumalaisamy Suryaprabha, Chunghyeon Choi, Yanfang Wu et al.. Smart wearable and implantable biosensors for continuous health monitoring: materials, biocompatibility, and AI integration. npj Flexible Electronics. 2026

This review examines smart wearable and implantable biosensors designed for continuous, real-time monitoring of biophysical and biochemical signals in the context of personalized and preventive healthcare. The paper surveys advances in flexible, stretchable, and biocompatible materials that enable long-term comfort and seamless integration with the body, alongside multimodal and multi-analyte sensing approaches that improve measurement robustness. It also reviews how artificial intelligence is being applied to enhance signal processing and generate predictive health insights from these devices. The review identifies persistent challenges including motion artifacts, energy autonomy, data privacy, and the clinical interpretability of AI-derived outputs. Overall, the paper consolidates current knowledge on materials, device architectures, and AI-assisted strategies as an integrated framework for next-generation biosensor development.

Data-Centric Foundation Models in Computational Healthcare: A Survey

Yunkun Zhang, Jin Gao, Zheling Tan et al.. Data-Centric Foundation Models in Computational Healthcare: A Survey. ACM Computing Surveys. 2026

This survey examines the intersection of foundation models and computational healthcare through a data-centric lens, focusing on how these AI systems can address longstanding challenges in clinical data acquisition, quality, and utilization. The authors review a broad range of data-centric approaches spanning the full pipeline from model pre-training through inference, with particular attention to issues of data quantity, annotation, patient privacy, and ethics. The survey synthesizes perspectives on AI security, model assessment, and alignment with human values as they apply to healthcare settings. The authors conclude with an outlook on how foundation model-based analytics could improve patient outcomes and clinical workflows, while providing a curated repository of relevant healthcare foundation models and datasets.

Gut microbiome and metabolic health: mechanisms and precision interventions

Zhengrui Li, Sudeshna Samui, J S Liu et al.. Gut microbiome and metabolic health: mechanisms and precision interventions. Gut Microbes. 2026

This review examines the gut microbiome as a central regulator of metabolic health, focusing on the mechanisms through which microbial communities influence energy balance, insulin sensitivity, inflammation, and inter-organ signaling. The authors synthesize evidence on key mediators including short-chain fatty acids, secondary bile acid signaling, gut barrier integrity, immune modulation, and the microbiota-gut-brain-pancreas axis, while also surveying disease-associated microbial alterations across obesity, type 2 diabetes, metabolic dysfunction-associated steatotic liver disease, and metabolic syndrome. Drawing on germ-free animal models, fecal microbiota transplantation studies, and strain-level interventions, they argue that shifts in microbial ecology can causally shape metabolic outcomes rather than merely reflecting them. The review critically evaluates therapeutic strategies such as targeted probiotics, prebiotics, dietary modulation, and fecal microbiota transplantation, and discusses why individual responses to these interventions vary. To unify these findings, the authors propose a precision microbiome intervention pipeline and a conceptual framework termed the Precision Microbiome Intervention Triangle, positioning the gut microbiome as both a mechanistic driver of metabolic disease and a modifiable target for personalized therapeutic design informed by multi-omics and machine learning approaches.

Tracing the Cosmic Origins: Machine Learning Reconstruction of the Primordial Density Field from EoR Observations

Anchal Saxena, P. Daniel Meerburg, Guochao Sun et al.. Tracing the Cosmic Origins: Machine Learning Reconstruction of the Primordial Density Field from EoR Observations. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper investigates whether the primordial density field of the Universe can be reconstructed from observations of the Epoch of Reionization, thereby recovering cosmological information lost to non-linear gravitational evolution and astrophysical processes. The authors used simulated 21-cm and CO line-intensity maps at high redshift, generated with the LIMFAST code, as inputs to a three-dimensional U-Net deep learning architecture trained to infer initial conditions at very high redshift. The two tracers were chosen because they probe complementary environments, with 21-cm emission sensitive to neutral low-density gas and CO tracing dense star-forming regions, and the analysis incorporated realistic instrumental effects mimicking next-generation surveys. The reconstruction quality, measured by cross-correlation with the true initial field, was highest when combining both tracers, achieving strong fidelity on intermediate and large scales even under noisy observational conditions. Crucially, performing simulation-based inference of cosmological parameters from power-spectrum summaries derived from the reconstructed fields tightened constraints on key parameters such as the amplitude of matter fluctuations and the spectral index by roughly a factor of two, demonstrating that joint multi-tracer reconstruction can recover cosmological information that would otherwise remain inaccessible.

WearableQA: A Benchmark for Health Reasoning over Real-World Wearable Data

Ji Soo Lee, Xilun Chen, Pierce Chuang et al.. WearableQA: A Benchmark for Health Reasoning over Real-World Wearable Data. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper introduces a benchmark called WearableQA designed to evaluate whether artificial intelligence systems can reason meaningfully over real-world longitudinal wearable sensor data from genuine users. The benchmark consists of thousands of multiple-choice questions derived from wearable time series, blood biomarkers, and demographic information spanning hundreds of days per user, preserving authentic data characteristics such as device noise and individual variability. To probe distinct reasoning capabilities, the benchmark organizes questions along two axes distinguishing computational data reasoning from physiological interpretation, and single-signal from multi-signal integration, supported by a dual-grounding framework combining literature-based findings with statistically validated population patterns. Evaluation across a range of both proprietary and open-source large language models revealed substantial variation in performance and demonstrated that the benchmark effectively differentiates model capabilities. Despite some models reaching moderate accuracy, the benchmark remains largely unsolved, indicating that current language models fall considerably short of robust health reasoning over real-world wearable data.

A Generalizable Feature Extractor for Alzheimer's-Related Brain MRI Tasks

Reza Rajabli, D. Louis Collins. A Generalizable Feature Extractor for Alzheimer's-Related Brain MRI Tasks. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper investigated whether a compact, supervised pretrained neural network could function as a generalizable foundation model for multiple Alzheimer's-related neuroimaging tasks. The researchers froze the weights of a three-dimensional convolutional neural network previously trained for brain-age prediction, then adapted it to various downstream tasks using Low-Rank Adaptation, which requires only a small fraction of additional trainable parameters. The approach was evaluated across six experiments spanning cognitive status classification, cross-cohort generalization without retraining, mild cognitive impairment progression prediction, amyloid positivity detection from structural MRI, and volumetric estimation of hippocampal and white matter hypointensity measures. The model demonstrated strong performance across all tasks, including transferring to an entirely unseen dataset without any additional training, and achieved volumetric estimation results comparable to much larger segmentation networks. The findings suggest that a carefully pretrained brain-age model can serve as an effective, reusable backbone for diverse Alzheimer's-related tasks even when labeled data are scarce.

MEOX: Compact Multimodal Mixture-of-Experts for Earth Observation

Mohanad Albughdadi. MEOX: Compact Multimodal Mixture-of-Experts for Earth Observation. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper introduces MEOX, a compact multimodal masked autoencoder designed for Earth observation that can handle heterogeneous sensors and missing data without requiring a large parameter count. The architecture uses sensor-specific adapters, validity signals, sparse mixture-of-experts blocks with shared and private low-rank components, and metadata tokens to enable modality-flexible processing and efficient parameter scaling. The model was pretrained on over a million multimodal satellite samples using masked reconstruction and structured sensor dropout, then evaluated on a suite of remote sensing classification and segmentation benchmarks through frozen and finetuned transfer. MEOX achieves competitive or superior performance compared to larger reported baselines on tasks including land cover segmentation and scene classification, demonstrating that strong sensor-flexible representations can be learned within a very compact parameter budget. Additional analyses including routing diagnostics, metadata ablations, and cross-sensor retrieval provide insight into how the model distributes function across experts and integrates multimodal information.

Mitigating Disease Spread by Design in Refugee and IDP Camps

Giulia Zarpellon, Joseph Aylett-Bullock, Frank Krauss et al.. Mitigating Disease Spread by Design in Refugee and IDP Camps. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper investigated how the physical layout of refugee and internally displaced person camps influences the spread of infectious diseases within those settlements. The researchers developed a methodology using an agent-based epidemic model to simulate how different camp designs affect the movement and interaction of residents, which in turn shapes disease transmission dynamics. By running proof-of-concept experiments, they demonstrated that camp layout can serve as a meaningful mitigating factor against disease spread. The work provides a framework for generating operational insights that could inform more health-conscious camp design in future humanitarian settings.

Does Your Agent's Memory Survive a Model Upgrade? A Controlled Study of Memory Portability

Ankit Goyal, Jaideep Ray. Does Your Agent's Memory Survive a Model Upgrade? A Controlled Study of Memory Portability. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper investigates whether the memory stores used by AI agents remain functional when the underlying language model is upgraded, a problem that has received little systematic attention despite model upgrades being commonplace. The researchers compared four memory representation strategies—verbatim long-context storage, retrieval-augmented generation with chunked text, model-compressed natural-language notes, and a fixed-schema knowledge graph—by testing how well each transferred across model swaps using synthetic conversation histories with verifiable answer codes. They found that fixed-schema knowledge graphs transferred with near-zero accuracy change, making them highly portable, while compressed notes exhibited strong coupling to the model that wrote them, with accuracy shifting asymmetrically and substantially depending on the direction of migration. In retrieval-augmented systems, using a mixed embedding index from partial re-embedding captured only a fraction of the accuracy gains achievable through full re-embedding, and diagnostic analysis revealed that most errors in compressed notes stemmed from information loss during initial construction rather than retrieval failure, while the opposite was true for retrieval-augmented generation. The study concludes that retaining original source histories is essential for effective memory repair after model upgrades, and that direction-specific migration testing and strict embedding space isolation are necessary practices for maintaining agent memory integrity.
